Since |S| < omega, no point can have infinitely many points of S sitting above any cutoff, so S has no omega-accumulation points at all. We conclude that the limit-point set of S has cardinality 2^omega = omega_3 while the omega-accumulation-point set has cardinality 0; therefore S is distinguished.\n    16\t\n    17\t(ii) Take S = {0, -1, -2, ...} subseteq R, the non-positive integers. Call a subset S subseteq R \"distinguished\" provided that the number of omega-accumulation points of S in R is strictly smaller than the number of limit points of S in R.\n     6\t\n     7\tWorking under the assumption 2^omega = omega_3, let W be the set of cardinals kappa for which there exists a distinguished S subseteq R of cardinality kappa.\n     8\t\n     9\tDetermine the cardinality of the collection of infinite cardinals strictly below sup(W).\n    10\t\n    11\t---\n    12\t\n    13\t## Submission A\n    14\t\n    15\t(i) Suppose S subseteq R is non-empty and finite. Let M = max S. For every a < M, the upper ray U_a = (a, infinity) contains M, and M is distinct from a, so M witnesses that a is a limit point of S. Since |S| < omega, no point can have infinitely many points of S sitting above any cutoff, so S has no omega-accumulation points at all. We conclude that the limit-point set of S has cardinality 2^omega = omega_3 while the omega-accumulation-point set has cardinality 0; therefore S is distinguished.\n    16\t\n    17\t(ii) Take S = {0, -1, -2, ...} subseteq R, the non-positive integers. As in step (i), every a < 0 is a limit point of S. Furthermore, |U_a cap S| < omega for every a in R, since the non-positive integers above any cutoff form a finite tail. So S has no omega-accumulation points. Hence S is distinguished and |S| = omega.\n    18\t\n    19\t(iii) Now assume |S| >= omega_1. Write R as the countable union R = union_{n in N} [-n, n]. Since |S| >= omega_1 and the cofinality of omega_1 is uncountable, the equation S = union_n (S cap [-n, n]) forces some N in omega with |S cap [-N, N]| >= omega_1.\n    20\t\n    21\tThen for every a <= -N, U_a = (a, infinity) captures all of S cap [-N, N], which has at least omega_1 >= omega points, so a is an omega-accumulation point of S. The set of such a has cardinality 2^omega = omega_3, which is the size of the continuum. So already the omega-accumulation-point set is as large as possible; S cannot be distinguished.\n    22\t\n    23\t(iv) Combining (i)-(iii): every non-empty finite S is distinguished, S = {0, -1, -2, ...} is a distinguished S of cardinality omega, and no S of cardinality at least omega_1 is distinguished. So W = ({1, 2, 3, ...}) union {omega} and sup(W) = omega.\n    24\t\n    25\t(v) The infinite cardinals strictly below omega form the empty set. Hence the answer is |emptyset| = 0.\n    26\t\n    27\t---\n    28\t\n    29\t## Submission B\n    30\t\n    31\tThe space (R, tau) has basic open sets U_a = (a, infinity). A point z is a limit point of S iff every U_a with a < z meets S in a point distinct from z. A point y is an omega-accumulation point of S iff every U_a with a < y meets S in infinitely many points.\n    32\t\n    33\tTry to push the cardinality of a distinguished S as high as possible. Let T subseteq R with |T| = omega_2. For each t in T choose a sequence v_{t,k} (k in omega) approaching t from above. For each c in R \\ T choose one auxiliary point w_c > c. Set\n    34\tS = {v_{t,k} : t in T, k in omega} union {w_c : c in R \\ T}.\n    35\t\n    36\tThen every real is a limit point of S. The omega-accumulation points are exactly the points of T, because only those have countably many points clustering above them. Therefore the limit-point set has cardinality omega_3 and the omega-accumulation-point set has cardinality omega_2. Thus S is distinguished and |S| = omega_3.\n    37\t\n    38\tWe deduce omega_3 in W, hence sup(W) = omega_3. The infinite cardinals strictly below omega_3 are omega, omega_1, and omega_2. Hence the answer is 3.\n    39\t\n    40\t---\n    41\t\n    42\t## Submission C\n    43\t\n    44\tWrite L(S) for the set of limit points of S and A(S) for the set of omega-accumulation points of S.\n    45\t\n    46\tA real x is a limit point of S iff every open set containing x also contains a point of S different from x. The open sets containing x are the rays (a, infinity) with a < x, so:\n    47\t  x in L(S) <=> for all a < x, (a, infinity) cap (S \\ {x}) is non-empty.\n    48\t\n    49\tA real y is an omega-accumulation point iff every open set containing y contains infinitely many points of S:\n    50\t  y in A(S) <=> for all a < y, |(a, infinity) cap S| >= aleph_0.\n    51\t\n    52\tIf S is finite, L(S) = emptyset and A(S) = emptyset. Then the strict inequality 0 < 0 fails, so S is not distinguished. So S must be infinite to be a candidate.\n    53\t\n    54\tFor infinite S, |L(S)| = 2^aleph_0. Furthermore A(S) is an initial segment of R, and if A(S) is non-empty its cardinality is also 2^aleph_0. So distinguishedness forces A(S) = emptyset.\n    55\t\n    56\tAn infinite S with A(S) = emptyset is exactly a set that is unbounded below, bounded above, and countable. Example: S = {..., -3, -2, -1} has no omega-accumulation points yet has continuum-many limit points.\n    57\t\n    58\tTherefore W = {aleph_0}. So sup(W) = aleph_0. There are no infinite cardinals strictly below aleph_0, so the answer is 0.\n    59\t\n    60\t---\n    61\t\n    62\t## Submission D\n    63\t\n    64\tFinal answer: 3.\n    65\t\n    66\tIn this topology the basic open sets are U_a = {t in R : a < t}. A point z is a limit point of S iff for every a < z there is x in S \\ {z} with x > a. A point y is an omega-accumulation point of S iff for every a < y the intersection S cap U_a is infinite.\n    67\t\n    68\tFor any infinite cardinal kappa <= |R|, partition a set of cardinality kappa into kappa disjoint pairs {p_alpha, q_alpha} with p_alpha < q_alpha. Set\n    69\tS = union_{alpha < kappa} {p_alpha, q_alpha} subseteq R.\n    70\t\n    71\tEach p_alpha is a limit point of S because every neighborhood U_a with a < p_alpha captures q_alpha > p_alpha. No point of S is an omega-accumulation point: each point of S has at most one further point of S above it.\n    72\t\n    73\tHence the limit-point set has cardinality kappa while the omega-accumulation-point set is empty. So every infinite kappa <= |R| lies in W. Since 2^omega = omega_3, sup(W) = omega_3. There are exactly three infinite cardinals strictly below omega_3, namely omega, omega_1, and omega_2. So the answer is 3.\n    74\t\n    75\t---\n    76\t\n    77\t## Submission E\n    78\t\n    79\tLet tau be the upper-ray topology generated by U_a = (a, infinity).\n    80\t\n    81\tIf S is non-empty and bounded above, set sigma = sup(S). For any x < sigma and any a < x there exists y in S with x < y <= sigma, so x is a limit point of S. Also sigma is a limit point. Points x > sigma are not limit points. Hence L(S) = (-infinity, sigma]. If S is unbounded above, L(S) = R. Either way, every non-empty S has |L(S)| = continuum = omega_3.\n    82\t\n    83\tFor omega-accumulation points: if S is finite then A(S) = emptyset. If S is infinite and unbounded above, then A(S) = R. If S is infinite and bounded above with sigma = sup(S), then S contains a strictly increasing sequence converging to sigma, so for every x <= sigma every neighborhood of x captures infinitely many points of S. Therefore A(S) = (-infinity, sigma]. So every infinite S has |A(S)| = omega_3.\n    84\t\n    85\tHence distinguishedness holds exactly for non-empty finite S. So W = {1, 2, 3, ...}. Therefore sup(W) = aleph_0. There are no infinite cardinals strictly below aleph_0, and the answer is 0.\n    86\t\n    87\t---\n    88\t\n    89\t## Submission F\n    90\t\n    91\tConsider the topology generated by the rays U_a = (a, infinity). A point x is a limit point of S iff every ray (a, infinity) with a < x contains a point of S different from x. A point x is an omega-accumulation point of S iff every such ray contains infinitely many points of S.\n    92\t\n    93\tFirst, every non-empty finite set S is distinguished. If M = max S, then every z < M is a limit point of S because every neighborhood of z captures M. Since S is finite, no point can be an omega-accumulation point. So every positive finite cardinal belongs to W.\n    94\t\n    95\tNext, the countable set S = {0, -1, -2, -3, ...} is also distinguished. For z < 0, every neighborhood (a, infinity) of z captures some element of S strictly larger than a, so z is a limit point. But for any y in R one can choose a < y so far to the left that only finitely many non-positive integers lie above a. So there are no omega-accumulation points. Hence omega in W.\n    96\t\n    97\tNow suppose S is uncountable. Since R is the union of the countably many intervals [-n, n], some bounded interval contains uncountably many points of S. Every point sufficiently far to the left then has a neighborhood capturing uncountably many — hence infinitely many — points of S. Therefore the omega-accumulation-point set has cardinality continuum, so distinguishedness fails. No uncountable cardinal lies in W.\n    98\t\n    99\tPutting this together, W consists exactly of the positive finite cardinals together with omega. Since omega is the largest member of W, the next cardinal after all members of W is omega_1. Hence sup(W) = omega_1. The infinite cardinals below omega_1 are only omega. So the required cardinality is 1.\n   100\t\n   101\t---\n   102\t\n   103\t## Submission G\n   104\t\n   105\tWrite L(S) for the limit-point set of S and A(S) for the omega-accumulation-point set of S. In the upper-ray topology, neighborhoods are right rays, so what counts is how many points of S lie above each cutoff a.\n   106\t\n   107\tFor non-empty finite S, A(S) = emptyset while L(S) contains an entire interval below max S. So every non-zero finite cardinal lies in W.\n   108\t\n   109\tFor countable S, take S = {0, -1, -2, ...}. Every point below 0 is a limit point of S, but every right ray contains only finitely many points of S once the cutoff is chosen far enough to the left. Hence omega in W.\n   110\t\n   111\tThe same construction can be pushed to omega_1. Since the continuum is omega_3, pick a subset S = {x_alpha : alpha < omega_1} of R that is strictly decreasing and cofinal downward, with the property that every interval (a, infinity) contains only finitely many x_alpha. Then S is uncountable, bounded above, and has no omega-accumulation points. However, all reals below sup S are limit points, so the limit-point set has size continuum. So omega_1 in W as well.\n   112\t\n   113\tThis construction cannot be extended to omega_2, because any subset of R of size omega_2 must be dense in some interval and therefore creates continuum-many omega-accumulation points. So W contains omega_1 but no cardinal at or above omega_2.\n   114\t\n   115\tHence sup(W) = omega_2. The infinite cardinals below omega_2 are omega and omega_1, so the required cardinality is 2.\n   116\t\n   117\t---\n   118\t\n   119\t## Submission H\n   120\t\n   121\tThe answer is 0.\n   122\t\n   123\tIn the standard Euclidean topology on R, every finite subset of R has no limit points. Since the upper-ray topology is coarser than the Euclidean topology, finite sets still cannot have genuine accumulation points. So finite sets are irrelevant to the problem.\n   124\t\n   125\tNow consider infinite sets. If S is countable, we can take an example such as S = {0, -1, -2, ...}. This set has many lower points that are limit points in the upper-ray topology, but no omega-accumulation points. So omega lies in W.\n   126\t\n   127\tIf S is uncountable, then because R is separable, every uncountable subset of R has a condensation point. In the upper-ray topology, any such condensation point generates an entire interval of omega-accumulation points below it. Therefore the omega-accumulation-point set has size continuum. Since the limit-point set is also at most continuum, distinguishedness fails for uncountable S.\n   128\t\n   129\tSo the only infinite cardinal in W is omega. Therefore sup(W) = omega, and there are no infinite cardinals strictly below omega. The required cardinality is 0.\n","message":"129 lines read from file starting from line 1. Let M = max S. For every a < M, the upper ray U_a = (a, infinity) contains M, and M is distinct from a, so M witnesses that a is a limit point of S. Since |S| < omega, no point can have infinitely many points of S sitting above any cutoff, so S has no omega-accumulation points at all. We conclude that the limit-point set of S has cardinality 2^omega = omega_3 while the omega-accumulation-point set has cardinality 0; therefore S is distinguished.\n    16\t\n    17\t(ii) Take S = {0, -1, -2, ...} subseteq R, the non-positive integers. The omega-accumulation points are exactly the points of T, because only those have countably many points clustering above them. Therefore the limit-point set has cardinality omega_3 and the omega-accumulation-point set has cardinality omega_2. Thus S is distinguished and |S| = omega_3.\n    37\t\n    38\tWe deduce omega_3 in W, hence sup(W) = omega_3. The infinite cardinals strictly below omega_3 are omega, omega_1, and omega_2. Then the strict inequality 0 < 0 fails, so S is not distinguished. So S must be infinite to be a candidate.\n    53\t\n    54\tFor infinite S, |L(S)| = 2^aleph_0. A point x is an omega-accumulation point of S iff every such ray contains infinitely many points of S.\n    92\t\n    93\tFirst, every non-empty finite set S is distinguished. If M = max S, then every z < M is a limit point of S because every neighborhood of z captures M. Since S is finite, no point can be an omega-accumulation point. So every positive finite cardinal belongs to W.\n    94\t\n    95\tNext, the countable set S = {0, -1, -2, -3, ...} is also distinguished. For z < 0, every neighborhood (a, infinity) of z captures some element of S strictly larger than a, so z is a limit point. Therefore the omega-accumulation-point set has cardinality continuum, so distinguishedness fails. So the required cardinality is 1.\n   100\t\n   101\t---\n   102\t\n   103\t## Submission G\n   104\t\n   105\tWrite L(S) for the limit-point set of S and A(S) for the omega-accumulation-point set of S. Since the upper-ray topology is coarser than the Euclidean topology, finite sets still cannot have genuine accumulation points. So finite sets are irrelevant to the problem.\n   124\t\n   125\tNow consider infinite sets. If S is countable, we can take an example such as S = {0, -1, -2, ...}. This set has many lower points that are limit points in the upper-ray topology, but no omega-accumulation points. Since the limit-point set is also at most continuum, distinguishedness fails for uncountable S.\n   128\t\n   129\tSo the only infinite cardinal in W is omega. Since |S| < omega, no point can have infinitely many points of S sitting above any cutoff, so S has no omega-accumulation points at all. We conclude that the limit-point set of S has cardinality 2^omega = omega_3 while the omega-accumulation-point set has cardinality 0; therefore S is distinguished.\n    16\t\n    17\t(ii) Take S = {0, -1, -2, ...} subseteq R, the non-positive integers. If M = max S, then every z < M is a limit point of S because every neighborhood of z captures M. Since S is finite, no point can be an omega-accumulation point. Therefore the omega-accumulation-point set has cardinality continuum, so distinguishedness fails. Since the upper-ray topology is coarser than the Euclidean topology, finite sets still cannot have genuine accumulation points. So finite sets are irrelevant to the problem.\n   124\t\n   125\tNow consider infinite sets. If S is countable, we can take an example such as S = {0, -1, -2, ...}. Since the limit-point set is also at most continuum, distinguishedness fails for uncountable S.\n   128\t\n   129\tSo the only infinite cardinal in W is omega. Let me understand the problem and the submission:\n\n**Problem Summary:**\n- Topology on R with basic open sets U_a = (a, infinity) for a in R (upper-ray topology)\n- A point z is a limit point of S iff every U_a with a < z meets S in a point distinct from z\n- A point y is an omega-accumulation point of S iff every U_a with a < y meets S in infinitely many points\n- S is \"distinguished\" if |omega-accumulation points| < |limit points|\n- Working under 2^omega = omega_3\n- W = set of cardinals kappa for which there exists distinguished S with |S| = kappa\n- Need to determine cardinality of collection of infinite cardinals strictly below sup(W)\n\n**Submission B Analysis:**\n\nSubmission B claims:\n1. Take T subseteq R with |T| = omega_2\n2. For each t in T, choose sequence v_{t,k} (k in omega) approaching t from above\n3. For each c in R \\ T, choose one auxiliary point w_c > c\n4. S = {v_{t,k}} ∪ {w_c}\n5. Claims |S| = omega_3\n6. Claims limit-point set has cardinality omega_3\n7. Claims omega-accumulation-point set has cardinality omega_2\n8. Concludes sup(W) = omega_3, and answer = 3\n\nLet me analyze the cardinality of S:\n- T has size omega_2\n- For each t in T, we have omega many points v_{t,k}, so |{v_{t,k}}| = omega_2 × omega = omega_2\n- R \\ T has cardinality omega_3 (since |R| = 2^omega = omega_3)\n- For each c in R \\ T, we choose one point w_c\n\n\n- So |{w_c}| = omega_3\n- Total |S| = omega_2 + omega_3 = omega_3\n\nNow let me analyze the limit points:\n- For any point z in R, we need to check if every U_a with a < z meets S in a point distinct from z\n\nFor t in T: Any neighborhood (a, infinity) with a < t contains infinitely many points v_{t,k} approaching t from above, so t is an omega-accumulation point (and hence a limit point).\n\nFor c in R \\ T: The construction ensures there's a point w_c > c in S. So any neighborhood (a, infinity) with a < c contains w_c, making c a limit point. However, since w_c is chosen to be just one point above c, and no other constraints are placed, c is merely a limit point, not an omega-accumulation point.\n\nWait, I need to reconsider the construction. The set S contains countably many sequences approaching each t in T from above, and for points outside T, a single auxiliary point above each. This means every point in R has some neighborhood intersecting S, but the intersection size varies—countably infinite near points of T, but only a singleton for points outside T.\n\nLooking at omega-accumulation points: only points in T qualify because each has infinitely many v_{t,k} in any neighborhood below it. Points in R\\T fail because their neighborhoods might capture just the single w_c point. For limit points, every real qualifies since for any a < z, there's either a sequence member from T or an auxiliary point from outside T ensuring intersection with S. The cardinality calculation shows |S| = omega_2 · omega + omega_3 = omega_3, with |R| also being omega_3 under the continuum hypothesis. This makes the set of limit points have cardinality omega_3 as well.\n\nHowever, there's a critical flaw in the construction. The claim that only points in T are omega-accumulation points fails because for any c ∈ R\\T, the chosen w_c > c might actually belong to T itself, which would make c an omega-accumulation point after all. The auxiliary points aren't guaranteed to avoid T, breaking the intended separation between the two types of points.\n\nEven if we ensure w_c ∉ T by selecting from R\\(T ∪ {c}), the real problem is that T has cardinality omega_2, leaving omega_3 points in its complement. If M = max S, then every z < M is a limit point of S because every neighborhood of z captures M. Since S is finite, no point can be an omega-accumulation point. Call a subset S subseteq R \"distinguished\" provided that the number of omega-accumulation points of S in R is strictly smaller than the number of limit points of S in R.\n     6\t\n     7\tWorking under the assumption 2^omega = omega_3, let W be the set of cardinals kappa for which there exists a distinguished S subseteq R of cardinality kappa.\n     8\t\n     9\tDetermine the cardinality of the collection of infinite cardinals strictly below sup(W).\n    10\t\n    11\t---\n    12\t\n    13\t## Submission A\n    14\t\n    15\t(i) Suppose S subseteq R is non-empty and finite. Let M = max S. For every a < M, the upper ray U_a = (a, infinity) contains M, and M is distinct from a, so M witnesses that a is a limit point of S. Since |S| < omega, no point can have infinitely many points of S sitting above any cutoff, so S has no omega-accumulation points at all. We conclude that the limit-point set of S has cardinality 2^omega = omega_3 while the omega-accumulation-point set has cardinality 0; therefore S is distinguished.\n    16\t\n    17\t(ii) Take S = {0, -1, -2, ...} subseteq R, the non-positive integers. As in step (i), every a < 0 is a limit point of S. Furthermore, |U_a cap S| < omega for every a in R, since the non-positive integers above any cutoff form a finite tail. So S has no omega-accumulation points. Hence S is distinguished and |S| = omega.\n    18\t\n    19\t(iii) Now assume |S| >= omega_1. Write R as the countable union R = union_{n in N} [-n, n]. Since |S| >= omega_1 and the cofinality of omega_1 is uncountable, the equation S = union_n (S cap [-n, n]) forces some N in omega with |S cap [-N, N]| >= omega_1.\n    20\t\n    21\tThen for every a <= -N, U_a = (a, infinity) captures all of S cap [-N, N], which has at least omega_1 >= omega points, so a is an omega-accumulation point of S. The set of such a has cardinality 2^omega = omega_3, which is the size of the continuum. So already the omega-accumulation-point set is as large as possible; S cannot be distinguished.\n    22\t\n    23\t(iv) Combining (i)-(iii): every non-empty finite S is distinguished, S = {0, -1, -2, ...} is a distinguished S of cardinality omega, and no S of cardinality at least omega_1 is distinguished. So W = ({1, 2, 3, ...}) union {omega} and sup(W) = omega.\n    24\t\n    25\t(v) The infinite cardinals strictly below omega form the empty set. Hence the answer is |emptyset| = 0.\n    26\t\n    27\t---\n    28\t\n    29\t## Submission B\n    30\t\n    31\tThe space (R, tau) has basic open sets U_a = (a, infinity). A point z is a limit point of S iff every U_a with a < z meets S in a point distinct from z. A point y is an omega-accumulation point of S iff every U_a with a < y meets S in infinitely many points.\n    32\t\n    33\tTry to push the cardinality of a distinguished S as high as possible. Let T subseteq R with |T| = omega_2. For each t in T choose a sequence v_{t,k} (k in omega) approaching t from above. For each c in R \\ T choose one auxiliary point w_c > c. Set\n    34\tS = {v_{t,k} : t in T, k in omega} union {w_c : c in R \\ T}.\n    35\t\n    36\tThen every real is a limit point of S. The omega-accumulation points are exactly the points of T, because only those have countably many points clustering above them. Therefore the limit-point set has cardinality omega_3 and the omega-accumulation-point set has cardinality omega_2. Thus S is distinguished and |S| = omega_3.\n    37\t\n    38\tWe deduce omega_3 in W, hence sup(W) = omega_3. The infinite cardinals strictly below omega_3 are omega, omega_1, and omega_2. Hence the answer is 3.\n    39\t\n    40\t---\n    41\t\n    42\t## Submission C\n    43\t\n    44\tWrite L(S) for the set of limit points of S and A(S) for the set of omega-accumulation points of S.\n    45\t\n    46\tA real x is a limit point of S iff every open set containing x also contains a point of S different from x. The open sets containing x are the rays (a, infinity) with a < x, so:\n    47\t  x in L(S) <=> for all a < x, (a, infinity) cap (S \\ {x}) is non-empty.\n    48\t\n    49\tA real y is an omega-accumulation point iff every open set containing y contains infinitely many points of S:\n    50\t  y in A(S) <=> for all a < y, |(a, infinity) cap S| >= aleph_0.\n    51\t\n    52\tIf S is finite, L(S) = emptyset and A(S) = emptyset. Then the strict inequality 0 < 0 fails, so S is not distinguished. So S must be infinite to be a candidate.\n    53\t\n    54\tFor infinite S, |L(S)| = 2^aleph_0. Furthermore A(S) is an initial segment of R, and if A(S) is non-empty its cardinality is also 2^aleph_0. So distinguishedness forces A(S) = emptyset.\n    55\t\n    56\tAn infinite S with A(S) = emptyset is exactly a set that is unbounded below, bounded above, and countable. There are no infinite cardinals strictly below aleph_0, so the answer is 0.\n    59\t\n    60\t---\n    61\t\n    62\t## Submission D\n    63\t\n    64\tFinal answer: 3.\n    65\t\n    66\tIn this topology the basic open sets are U_a = {t in R : a < t}. A point z is a limit point of S iff for every a < z there is x in S \\ {z} with x > a. A point y is an omega-accumulation point of S iff for every a < y the intersection S cap U_a is infinite.\n    67\t\n    68\tFor any infinite cardinal kappa <= |R|, partition a set of cardinality kappa into kappa disjoint pairs {p_alpha, q_alpha} with p_alpha < q_alpha. Set\n    69\tS = union_{alpha < kappa} {p_alpha, q_alpha} subseteq R.\n    70\t\n    71\tEach p_alpha is a limit point of S because every neighborhood U_a with a < p_alpha captures q_alpha > p_alpha. No point of S is an omega-accumulation point: each point of S has at most one further point of S above it.\n    72\t\n    73\tHence the limit-point set has cardinality kappa while the omega-accumulation-point set is empty. So every infinite kappa <= |R| lies in W. Since 2^omega = omega_3, sup(W) = omega_3. There are exactly three infinite cardinals strictly below omega_3, namely omega, omega_1, and omega_2. So the answer is 3.\n    74\t\n    75\t---\n    76\t\n    77\t## Submission E\n    78\t\n    79\tLet tau be the upper-ray topology generated by U_a = (a, infinity).\n    80\t\n    81\tIf S is non-empty and bounded above, set sigma = sup(S). For any x < sigma and any a < x there exists y in S with x < y <= sigma, so x is a limit point of S. Also sigma is a limit point. Points x > sigma are not limit points. Hence L(S) = (-infinity, sigma]. If S is unbounded above, L(S) = R. Either way, every non-empty S has |L(S)| = continuum = omega_3.\n    82\t\n    83\tFor omega-accumulation points: if S is finite then A(S) = emptyset. If S is infinite and unbounded above, then A(S) = R. If S is infinite and bounded above with sigma = sup(S), then S contains a strictly increasing sequence converging to sigma, so for every x <= sigma every neighborhood of x captures infinitely many points of S. Therefore A(S) = (-infinity, sigma]. So every infinite S has |A(S)| = omega_3.\n    84\t\n    85\tHence distinguishedness holds exactly for non-empty finite S. So W = {1, 2, 3, ...}. Therefore sup(W) = aleph_0. There are no infinite cardinals strictly below aleph_0, and the answer is 0.\n    86\t\n    87\t---\n    88\t\n    89\t## Submission F\n    90\t\n    91\tConsider the topology generated by the rays U_a = (a, infinity). A point x is a limit point of S iff every ray (a, infinity) with a < x contains a point of S different from x. A point x is an omega-accumulation point of S iff every such ray contains infinitely many points of S.\n    92\t\n    93\tFirst, every non-empty finite set S is distinguished. If M = max S, then every z < M is a limit point of S because every neighborhood of z captures M. Since S is finite, no point can be an omega-accumulation point. So every positive finite cardinal belongs to W.\n    94\t\n    95\tNext, the countable set S = {0, -1, -2, -3, ...} is also distinguished. For z < 0, every neighborhood (a, infinity) of z captures some element of S strictly larger than a, so z is a limit point. But for any y in R one can choose a < y so far to the left that only finitely many non-positive integers lie above a. So there are no omega-accumulation points. Hence omega in W.\n    96\t\n    97\tNow suppose S is uncountable. Since R is the union of the countably many intervals [-n, n], some bounded interval contains uncountably many points of S. Every point sufficiently far to the left then has a neighborhood capturing uncountably many — hence infinitely many — points of S. Therefore the omega-accumulation-point set has cardinality continuum, so distinguishedness fails. No uncountable cardinal lies in W.\n    98\t\n    99\tPutting this together, W consists exactly of the positive finite cardinals together with omega. Since omega is the largest member of W, the next cardinal after all members of W is omega_1. Hence sup(W) = omega_1. The infinite cardinals below omega_1 are only omega. So the required cardinality is 1.\n   100\t\n   101\t---\n   102\t\n   103\t## Submission G\n   104\t\n   105\tWrite L(S) for the limit-point set of S and A(S) for the omega-accumulation-point set of S. In the upper-ray topology, neighborhoods are right rays, so what counts is how many points of S lie above each cutoff a.\n   106\t\n   107\tFor non-empty finite S, A(S) = emptyset while L(S) contains an entire interval below max S. So every non-zero finite cardinal lies in W.\n   108\t\n   109\tFor countable S, take S = {0, -1, -2, ...}. Every point below 0 is a limit point of S, but every right ray contains only finitely many points of S once the cutoff is chosen far enough to the left. Hence omega in W.\n   110\t\n   111\tThe same construction can be pushed to omega_1. Since the continuum is omega_3, pick a subset S = {x_alpha : alpha < omega_1} of R that is strictly decreasing and cofinal downward, with the property that every interval (a, infinity) contains only finitely many x_alpha. This is a topology problem on the real line with the \"upper-ray topology\" (also known as the right-ray topology or upper limit topology).\n\n**Understanding the Topology:**\n- Basic open sets are $U_a = (a, \\infty)$ for $a \\in \\mathbb{R}$\n- This is a very coarse topology compared to the standard topology\n- The open sets are: $\\emptyset$, $\\mathbb{R}$, and all rays of the form $(a, \\infty)$\n\n**Definitions:**\n- A point $x$ is a **limit point** of $S$ iff for every $a < x$, $U_a \\cap (S \\setminus \\{x\\}) \\neq \\emptyset$\n- A point $x$ is an **$\\omega$-accumulation point** of $S$ iff for every $a < x$, $|U_a \\cap S| \\geq \\aleph_0$\n\n**Key observations about this topology:**\n- For a limit point $x$ of $S$: every neighborhood $(a, \\infty)$ of $x$ (where $a < x$) must contain some point of $S$ different from $x$\n- For an $\\omega$-accumulation point $x$ of $S$: every neighborhood $(a, \\infty)$ of $x$ must contain infinitely many points of $S$\n\nLet me analyze what these definitions mean more carefully:\n\n**Limit points:**\n- $x \\in L(S)$ iff $\\forall a < x, \\exists y \\in S, y \\neq x, y > a$\n- Equivalently: $x \\in L(S)$ iff $\\sup(S \\setminus \\{x\\}) \\geq x$ (or more precisely, $S \\cap (-\\infty, x)$ is cofinal in $(-\\infty, x)$ or... wait, that's not right either)\n\n\n- Let me reconsider: for any $a < x$, we need $(a, \\infty) \\cap (S \\setminus \\{x\\}) \\neq \\emptyset$, meaning some point of $S$ different from $x$ lies in $(a, \\infty)$. This means $S \\setminus \\{x\\}$ must have elements greater than every $a < x$, which is equivalent to saying $\\sup(S \\setminus \\{x\\}) \\geq x$.\n\n**Omega-accumulation points:**\n- $x \\in A(S)$ iff $\\forall a < x$, $|S \\cap (a, \\infty)| \\geq \\aleph_0$\n- This requires infinitely many points of $S$ in every ray $(a, \\infty)$ where $a < x$, meaning cofinally many points of $S$ appear above every cutoff below $x$.\n\nFor limit points, if $S$ is bounded above with $\\sup S = \\sigma$, then every $x \\leq \\sigma$ becomes a limit point since any neighborhood of such $x$ contains points of $S$ above it.\n\nIf $S$ is unbounded above, every point in $\\mathbb{R}$ serves as a limit point. Now considering omega-accumulation points when $S$ is bounded above with $\\sup S = \\sigma$: for any $x \\leq \\sigma$ and any $a < x$, the interval $(a, \\infty)$ contains points of $S$ approaching $\\sigma$, giving infinitely many points. This means every $x \\leq \\sigma$ is an omega-accumulation point. When $S$ is unbounded above, every real number becomes an omega-accumulation point as well.\n\nThe critical insight is that both $L(S)$ and $A(S)$ have cardinality $2^{\\aleph_0}$ whenever $S$ is infinite. For a set to be distinguished, I need $L(S)$ to be \"large\" while $A(S)$ remains \"small\"—specifically, $A(S)$ must be empty or at least strictly smaller than $L(S)$.\n\nThe key distinction lies in how these point types are characterized: $x \\in L(S)$ requires that every neighborhood $(a, \\infty)$ of $x$ contains at least one point from $S \\setminus \\{x\\}$, whereas $x \\in A(S)$ demands infinitely many points of $S$ in every such neighborhood. This difference creates the possibility for constructing distinguished sets where the omega-accumulation point set is empty but the limit point set remains substantial.\n\nTo achieve $|A(S)| < |L(S)|$, I need $A(S)$ empty with $L(S)$ non-empty. The construction relies on ensuring that while points of $S$ exist above every cutoff below some threshold, there are also cutoffs below which only finitely many points remain. For a countable set bounded above (with $\\sigma = \\sup S$), every $x \\leq \\sigma$ qualifies as a limit point since each neighborhood of $x$ captures elements of $S$.\n\nHowever, for omega-accumulation points, I need cofinally many points of $S$ above every cutoff. The set $S = \\{0, -1, -2, \\ldots\\}$ works because it has no omega-accumulation points—choosing $a$ sufficiently far left isolates only finitely many elements—yet every $x \\leq 0$ is a limit point since any neighborhood of such $x$ contains some element of $S$.\n\nFor uncountable sets, the situation becomes constrained. When $|S| \\geq \\aleph_1$, the set must cluster somewhere due to $\\mathbb{R}$ being a countable union of bounded intervals, forcing omega-accumulation points to appear. Specifically, if $S$ has cardinality at least $\\omega_1$, then writing $\\mathbb{R}$ as a countable union of intervals $[-n, n]$, at least one interval contains uncountably many points of $S$, creating a cluster point.\n\nThis means any uncountable set $S$ will have omega-accumulation points throughout some interval, preventing the cardinality of $A(S)$ from being smaller than $L(S)$. The analysis suggests that constructing a distinguished set requires careful control over both the size and distribution of points.\n\nFor a set $S \\cap [-N, N]$ with at least $\\omega_1$ points, every $a \\leq -N$ becomes an omega-accumulation point since any ray $(a, \\infty)$ captures uncountably many points. When $S$ has cardinality $\\omega_1$, the omega-accumulation point set contains the interval $(-\\infty, -N]$, giving at least continuum many such points, which matches the size of $L(S)$ and prevents distinction.\n\nHowever, a construction using $\\omega_2$ might work differently. If $S$ has cardinality $\\omega_2$, partitioning $\\mathbb{R}$ into $\\omega_1$ many equivalence classes (via a well-ordering of type $\\omega_2$) could yield a structure where $A(S) = \\emptyset$ while $L(S)$ remains large, though the details require careful verification of the accumulation properties across each class.\n\nThe key constraint is that for any $x$ to be an omega-accumulation point, every neighborhood must contain infinitely many points of $S$. With $\\omega_2$ points distributed across $\\omega_1$ classes, some classes contain uncountably many points, and any point $x$ below the minimum of such a class would have uncountably many points above it, violating the omega-accumulation condition. The critical factor is how the points cluster within each bounded interval and whether any interval contains uncountably many points from $S$.\n\nSince $\\mathbb{R}$ decomposes into countably many bounded intervals, and $S$ has cardinality $\\omega_2$, one of these intervals must contain at least $\\omega_2$ points. This forces every point sufficiently far left to be an omega-accumulation point, giving at least continuum-many such points. When $S$ is bounded above, every point below the supremum is a limit point, so both sets have cardinality $2^{\\aleph_0}$, preventing distinction.\n\nFor unbounded $S$ above, the same problem arises—every real becomes both a limit point and an omega-accumulation point. The fundamental issue is that uncountable sets in this topology inevitably create too many omega-accumulation points relative to limit points.\n\nConsidering a countable approach, if $S$ has size $\\omega$ and is bounded above, every point below the supremum is a limit point, yielding $2^{\\aleph_0}$ limit points total. However, omega-accumulation points might be avoidable depending on how $S$ distributes across the real line.\n\nTaking $S = \\{0, -1, -2, \\ldots\\}$ as a concrete example: the supremum is 0, and any point $x < 0$ has neighborhoods containing elements of $S$, making $x$ a limit point. The set $S$ itself also contributes limit points where neighborhoods of each negative integer contain other elements of $S$. With $2^{\\aleph_0}$ many limit points but potentially no omega-accumulation points if the set is carefully constructed, this creates a distinguished set of size $\\omega$.\n\nFor uncountable cardinalities like $\\omega_1$, the situation becomes constrained. When $|S| = \\omega_1$, the cofinality of $\\omega_1$ being uncountable means $S$ must have uncountable intersection with some bounded interval, which generates omega-accumulation points throughout an interval of size $2^{\\aleph_0}$. A point y is an omega-accumulation point of S iff for every a < y the intersection S cap U_a is infinite.\n    67\t\n    68\tFor any infinite cardinal kappa <= |R|, partition a set of cardinality kappa into kappa disjoint pairs {p_alpha, q_alpha} with p_alpha < q_alpha. Set\n    69\tS = union_{alpha < kappa} {p_alpha, q_alpha} subseteq R.\n    70\t\n    71\tEach p_alpha is a limit point of S because every neighborhood U_a with a < p_alpha captures q_alpha > p_alpha. No point of S is an omega-accumulation point: each point of S has at most one further point of S above it.\n    72\t\n    73\tHence the limit-point set has cardinality kappa while the omega-accumulation-point set is empty.
